TO  
Sir E. A. GAIT, K.C.S.I., C.I.E., I.C.S.,  
the keen Ethnologist, who during the many years he was connected with  
the administration of the Mundas' country as Commissioner, as Chief  
Secretary to the Government of Bihar and Orissa and finally as Lieutenant-  
Governor of the same Province, always showed a most sympathetic interest  
in the welfare of the Mundas and the other Aborigines of Chota Nagpur,  
this work is respectfully dedicated.

PREFACE,  
Mundari is spoken by at most 600,000 people surviving mainly in Chota  
Nagpur and in the Assam and Bhutan tea gardens, and these take more and  
more to the Aryan Hindi and Sadani, and the “total number of Aborigines  
speaking dialects of the Munda family of speech hardly amounts to  
3,200,000. Since the Mundas cannot continue much longer as a separate  
people, their language is doomed to disappear altogether; from the list of  
living languages in a comparatively short time, The only non-Mundas  
taking a sufficient interest in it to try and learn it, are some thirty missionaries, half a dozen officials and, perhaps a stray planter here and there.  
A dictionary of several volumes for such a language, will no doubt appear  
uncalled for and, from the mere business point of view, as overexpensive  
if not a downright waste of Government money.  
But that which, from this point of view, might be urged against the  
work, is precisely its strongest justification. Just because Mundari is  
a rapidly disappearing language, no amount of money, work or energy ought  
to be considered too great a price for a record of it that is as complete as  
possible. ‘The loss to the comparative study of Indo-Germanic languages  
would not be very serious, if one or the other germanic, celtic or slavonic  
dialects had tracelessly disappeared, because the number of studied dialects  
suffices for the purposes of comparative philology. But the same cannot  
be said of that vast family of languages, lying outside of the” monosyllabic,  
the Semitic and the Indo-Germanie families. So little is, up to date, really  
known about this family, that it has received a new name neatly every  
decade since the rise of comparative philology. The greater number of  
these languages arc, even now, but half-explored or entirely unknown, and  
what is worse, a number of them disappeared altogether, before a record of  
them was made. It is impossible to correctly gauge the loss caused thereby  
to science. Connexions which might have been easily perceived, can now  
no longer be demonstrated ; they can at best be conjectured from indirect  
and hardly sufficient indices. To give but one example :: The brothers  
Paul and Friedrich Sarasin and C. G. Seligman give short lists of words  
used by the Ceylon Veddas, which seem not to have been taken over together  
with the Singhalese language, but do apparently belong to their own original

Tad wo a tolerab!y complete record of the language of these Ccylonese  
eave dwellers, whe, even now-a-days, gain the whole of their animal food  
by the chase and the whole of their vegetable food from the roots, leaves,  
grains and fruits of self-sown plants, we could {probably infer from it, that  
the Mundas were still in living contact with the Veddas,. and that they are  
_fither a more alvaneed branch of the same race, or that they found them  
established already in parts of India, when they themselves came first to  
that country, and we could thus judgy how far they influenced each other.  
However that may be, if comparative philology is to succeed in giving  
a really satisfactory comparison of still surviving languages of that vast  
complex of interrelated languages, which extené over @ very great part of  
the globe, it can ill afford to lore even a single syllable of Mundari; for  
some of its features can hardly be found in the same explicit manner in any  
of the surviving languages. Its very characteristic structure points to  
a hoary antiquity, and it offers unmistakable affinities with other unexplored  
dialects or branches, All this fits in to throw much light on many dark  
points in the immense ficld opening out here before the comparative science  
of languages.  
On the other hand, we meet in Mundari with a great number of so-called  
Aryan roots. That many of tlesc should belong to Sanskrit and other  
related Indian languages, is not surprising. But it is very surprising that  
quite a number of them, which are not met with in the Indian branches of  
the Indo-Germanie languages, should be found in the non-Indian languages  
of that family. This raises the interesting question : are we not obliged to  
odmit that, both, the Aryan Janguages and the family (0 which Mundati

Tf a complete record of Mundari words contributes to the solution of  
the above (question, the trouble spent on it, will not have been taken in:vaine-  
For th’s reason great pains have been taken to point out all the words, which  
Mundari has in common with the languages of such Indian races as are or  
were in contact with the Mundas. It has not been possible to take in all  
the Indian dialects and langaages, but through the kind services of Jesuit  
missionaries in differeut parts of India, the following have bsen examined:  
(1) among the Aryan langnagos: Sanskrit, Hindi, Sadani. Occasional  
references to Hindustani are noted with A. standing) for Arabie, P. for  
Persian, U, for Urdu and Mt. for Marati, (2) Dravidian languages are  
represented by Oraon, marked Or., Singhalese, marked Sinh,, and Tamil  
marked T. Hven for these exhaustivencss cannot be vouched for. It is,  
however, hoped that the work will present the enquirer witha residne of  
words, which in their majority are paroly Mundari, i.e, words of which it  
oan be maintained, that the Mundas have not taken them from other known  
Ludian sources. Ife cin therefore conpare them with non-Aryan'and non-  
Dravidian words without fear of wasting|time over comparisons that would  
lead to no conclusions or, at best, to-doultful ones. Since this/has beca  
the ouly aim in these comparisons, no attompt has ben made, to ascortain,  
‘who was the borrower. This, however, does not imply the admission, that  
the Mundas haye, in all these cases, borrowed from the Aryans or Dravidians,  
Since the latter came into India after the Mandas, it may ba considered as  
evident, that they borrowed certain terms from the Mundas, especially names  
of plants and products found only there. This is all the more probable,  
because Mundari has desply influznood the stractucal character of the  
various Aryan languages, which arose and developed out of Sanskrit in India 5.  
afact which shows that in the beginning great nambers of the Aborigines,,  
mingled freely with and were absorbed by the immigrant Aryans.  
Furthermore, this residue of Mundari words may be assumed as  
a basis forthe comparison of Mundari with other dialects of the family  
such as Santali, Ho, Bhumij, Turi, Kharia in India and of others ont of  
India, for the purpose of ascertaining the laws regulating the phonetic  
changes in the various dialectse I found no time for this fascinating work  
whilst I was in India, and now, that a forced abszne2 from that oountty  
has made it altogether impossible for me, I can only hope, that some among  
those, who remain there, may take it up and carry it to a successful ¢onolu-  
sion, This hope of mine is the main reason for whieb all iomeaionel  
comparisons have been excluded.  
Those comparisons with other languages, which have been made, are tod  
few ta appreciably affect the bulk of the work, which calls for an’ explanation

## Scan page 15

vi  
and, to come extent, also for an apology. For, sinze the, pucély intellectual  
and material civilization of the Mundas is so very limited, one might  
natorally expect, that the number of their words should be correspondingly  
limited also. That would be so indeed, if their language had developed  
along the same lines as that of the so-called organic languages. But in  
more than one essential point it follows quite different lines, and, strange  
as this may appear at first right, their mode of proceeding exacts @ much  
Inrger number of words than onr own. This copiousness is not perfection  
but an imperfection, and the comparatively small number of words in an  
organic language is no sign of poverty and weakness, but rather a proof of  
internal perfection and power, us the following considerations will show  
I. We express whole categories of living beings and inanimate objacts by  
a single common noun, and then distinguish them by means of specifying  
adjectives, Thus we uso the worl, “basket” to denote all shapes and  
sizes of this picee of domestic furnitare, and we distinguish b.tween them  
by saying, v-g-, a square, a round, a high, a low, a largo, ® small, a tinye  
narrow-nocked, etc. basket. The Mundas on the contrary have quit,  
Ristinct words to denote baskets of different sizes and shapes: udila, kunci,  
falsi, dali (PI. XV1), Farka, diliggi, batka (Pl. XX), piti (UL XXMM1),  
Juki, Lagi (Pl XV), dan{i (Pl X). They follow the same practice  
for many of their utensils and weapons, cfr. the names of arrows ant  
weapons on Pl. TI, 11 and 1V-  
Similarly they use quite distinct words for forms or modes of aotivily  
which we denote by a single common verb or abstract noun,  
The reason of this decp d'fference in the developmont of the two kinds of  
language lies in the following fact: In all those elementary operations  
of the intellect, which lic at the beginning of Ianguage-building, the mind  
of the Aborigines works with a most astonishing precision Proofs of this  
pro met with at every turn in Mundari. Beyond these elementary opera-  
tions begins that higher sphere of intellectuality, in which the mind trics  
by personal, i-c., solf-willed efforts, to work out and clearly conceive the end,  
or purposes of his own creation, and to devise means for attaining them,  
To this class of mental creations belong our abstract generic and spcciflo  
nouns, which classify concepts into distinct eategories conceived by the mind  
for the purpose of simplification. In other words, our mind replaces the  
multiplicity of concrete terms by a small number of generic and spccific  
ones. Thereby language is transformed into an instrument which, in a com-  
_ paratively simple furm, increases (he facility of handling the most compli-  
cated thoughts,

vil  
Perhaps partly out of sheer indolence, but more likely on scsount of  
the great facility with which the Aborigines of certain climes, find all their  
simple lives stand in need of, these Aborigines are not 50 strongly urged  
or force to intense intellectual activity, snd therfore, they are satisfied  
to stick to the conorcte terms they have coined. They find it easier to tax  
their memory than to take to the more arduous labour of higher and purcr  
intellectuality. The inevitable consequence of this is of course, that stag-  
nation, for thousands of years, in that low state of intellectual and material  
culture, which appeats 80 incomprelinsible to us. Unfortunately this  
astonishment of ours leads so many to the orroncous conclusion, that their  
morality must be on the same low level. The well ascertained fact that all  
Aborigines uot oorrupted! by foreign influences, haves a Ligh standard of  
morality, and the equally well established fact, that low and the lowest  
morality in every scuse of the word so often disgraces the highest jutellee~  
tual and material forms of civilization, onght to have put us on our guard  
long ago against such a fatal error of judgment-  
II. Mundari transitive and intransitive predicates have 4 voices, 4 moody  
and 21 tenses. 1 might of course refer the student to my Mandari  
Grammar, which gives euflicient explanations about ull these intricacies, to  
enable a» keen student to extricate himself from most of them. I have  
hesitated a long time before 1 decid od not totdo so. The uses of the reflexive  
the reciprocal and the passive voice-forms are so strongly idivmatio, that it  
is frequently impossible for non-Munda, o guess what may be then and  
there actually meant by thom, Th» same is the éase with the compommds  
obtained by inflxing x after the first yowel of the predicate and then repeat.  
ing the vowel and adding to it the remainder of the word, vg, actt,'a-n-acw 5  
rakuh, ra-n-aka}. Therefore {hose 4 forms are always added to the active  
mood-form with their actual meavings and idiomatic uses. This causes of  
course a considerable lengthening of the work. But without that it would  
be of little help to those, who arc obliged to acquire a practical kngwledge  
of the language.  
ILL. Another class of words taking up much space, are nearly entirely  
wanting in our lapguages, namely, the s2-called jingles. They generally  
perform the function of adverbs and adjectives. Here it is no longer the  
want of ready-made words for those two functions, which forced the Mundas  
to coin these jingles. For they are as well provided with the usual adverbs of  
manner, time, place, ete., as we are; and sinoe each of their 21 tenee-forma  
say also perform the functions of adjectives and adverbs, they are in this  
respect, even better off than we are. What then is the reason, which urged  
them to add now words to the great number of adverbs belonging to tus

## Scan page 17

* vi  
tivo classes just mentioned, which do so adequately denote all the mplifica-  
tivns generally predicated of states and actions? ‘The only one «assignable  
is the following: The Munda feels the need of words, which superadd to ,  
the usual manner of modifying etates and actions, the connotation of the  
various feelings of pleasure, delight, wonder or displeasure, annoyance, grief  
cr horror, caused by the particular manner in which certain actions are  
performed. At the root of this there seems to lic something akin to the  
cause, which makes him fcol satisfied with his crowd of concrete terms,  
rather than trouble about creating general nouns and yerbs, A certain  
amount of intellectual indolenes, together with a kind of childish pleasuro  
he feels in vivid appeals to his imagination, makes him prefer these terms  
for ordinary use. It*is probably the same reason, which impels him to name  
so many birds and animals by words imitating their ery, rather than coining  
names denoting some characteristic quality which a close, and therefore more  
troublesome obscrvation and generalisation, has noticed in them.  
These jingles have, as a rale no ascertainable mean’ng in and by them-  
selves. In many cases they are ovidently created by the childish desire of  
imitative harmony, v.g., cerebere, usod of the twittering of many birds:  
rayaibayat, used of the confused noise ciusel by a crowd of people, all  
talking at the same time, The few terms of the kind, surviving in our  
Ianguages, do nearly all belong to this class. The majority of Mundari  
jingles do however not belong to it. They act dircetly by their rhythm.  
This is produced either (1) by the repetition of the sam» sound, as pirpir,  
Jilif-jilit, To these the present-tensa ‘aflix fan is often aldod with an  
intervening vowel which, while cuphonising the contact of two hard conso-  
nants, keeps up the rhythm, as pirpirééax ; or by the addition of such vowel  
to the first or the socond sound, as, jilifcjilis, or, jilrp-jilibe ; (2) by the  
addition of a second term, harmonising with the first. These additions show  
either a mere vowel change, as, daadagkuaduy, or a more change of consonant  
as kelexbeler, or both a vowel an] a consonant change, as, kerebore. These  
seom to be imitative in a mach wider sense, i.e., not of sound only, but of  
all kinds of impressions produ.ed through the scnses of sight, taste, sincll  
and touch. In fact, there is hardly any natural phenomenon producing  
distinctly pleasing, charming, dolightfal or disagreeable, painful, or distress-  
ing impressions, which has not a corresponding jingle. It seems as though  
these Aborigines had kept alive a wider sense or instinct of imitating all kinds  
of feelings by thythmic'sounds, which we have lost.  
IV. When the Mundas borrow a word from another language they do;  
like all borrowers the world over, give to it that form, which the phonetic  
Jaws of their own languige demand, and so, the English school, becomes:  
skit, the Sansksit, op, becomes, af, the Dravidian, a-wul, Locomes, acals,

“All ‘this is generally expressed by the term, to Mundarize.  
ix  
Bat the Mundas do ndt merely Mundarize the outer form or the boily  
of the borrowed words, ‘they also Mun darize their soul, i..o,, their function or  
signifying powor. They ‘treat them in every respeet as thoughithey were

those words are gouerally restricted to one single grammatical function ;  
thus, v.g., the Hindi a/gana is a verb and means to separate, to loosen. The  
Munda is not satisfied with changing its form into 2/yad or merely, a’ga ho  
loosens or frees ‘his form also from the function to which the Hindi form  
is tied down and troats it functionally just as le does his own original  
words. Ife makes the borrowed words perform the function of nouns,

bwists thom, 80 to say, into idioms of his own, of which a European would  
agver think, and the meaning of which he could not even guess, if they,  
* “Wore not explained to him. And a0 iteomes to pass that a simple Hindi  
advorb, taken over by the Mundas, forces the lexicographer to write a whole  
page in order to show, in an intelligible mannor, all that the Mundas make

a number of pages in this work.  
Finally, a last reason, which'adds more to the size of the work than the  
four preoeeding ones together, may be called a sentimental hobby of my,  
own: the Mandas say, that he who sncrifices a human being, is obliged to  
worship the victim’s spirit daring the rest of his life, to componsate him for  
the worship be -would have received from his own desceridents; had'he been  
allowed to live hisnormal life. I'am convinced that the Aryans are mainly  
answerable for the impending extinction of the Munda race as such partly  
by unjustifiable persecutions and ‘crutlties, partly by ealumnics and a con-  
temptuous neglect of interest in their civilization, and partly’ by a more or.  
less inculpable misunderstanding of that civilization. It isthertfore a duty  
incumbent on our race, that some rember of it should try to give as’ fuithfal  
a pictiure of that civilization as possible, and thereby keep alive at least the  
memory of that, which hasbeen: so riithlessly add so senselesely destroyed.  
For that ‘reason I have resolved to incorporate into this work all'that’ I have  
lear frou tho-Mandas abou thet voonemic, seis, moral and fligious life

‘ x  
as fully as Tam able, And this accounts forthe pretentious title, .Lagyclo=  
predia Mundarica. Whilst I believe that most of what I offer onthese matters  
is unknown to and partly even unsuspected by the majority of Europeans,  
T have no doubt that much of it is alrealy known to ethnologiets, and there,  
fore I lay myself opon to the chargs of adding useless ballast to the ethno-  
graphic an] ethaologic literature. My s'ncere love for the Mundas and my

excuse I plead to thal charge. The features of the Munda race are humane,  
earnest and sympathetic enough to deserve a place in the album of humanity.  
Tn ordor to acknowledge the most valuable assistance given to me in  
various ways, I must briefly explain how my Mundari vocabulary devolopod  
gradually to its present size: In 1838 I started collesting materials for a  
Mandari dictionary by culling and arranging in alphabetical order all the  
words I met in the then most scanty Mandari literature. This was limited  
{oa translation of the four gospels by the German Evangelical Gosner  
Mission in Ranchi, a Mundari Grammar by the Rev. Mr.! Notrott of the  
game Mission, a few grammatical notes by Rev. Mr. Whitley of the Anglican  
Mission. To this neither rich nor very reliable material, was added a  
Catholic catechism then in preparation, and a collection of tomo SQ letters  
written by children of the Catholic orphanage in Chaibassa. What I Iu.  
thas collected I put at the disposal of Rev. ‘Theophilus Bodson S.J. when he  
went to Ranchi in 1891, whore he continued collecting materials, These  
he started lithographing and had finished the letters A, B, C and D, when  
he died in Junc 1892. OF the remaining letters he left only Mundari_ words  
on small tickets. Those were made over to mo together with a small note-

quasi stonographic style for his own personal use, a certain number of Mundari  
expressions and sentences. As I was myself sent to Chota Nagpar in 18/2,  
I continued my own collection and kept working at ite completion with such  
continuity as my missionary work allowed me. A few years after the  
publication of my Mundari grammar by the Government of Bengal, Mr. F,

lary by a member of the Germin Evangelical Mission, with a request to  
state, whether I thought it worth being printed by Government. I returned  
it unopened with the remark that, as I was myself preparing a Mundari  
dictionary, I preferred not to sec it, in order to avoid the suspicion of having  
used it for my own work. Thereupon he replied that, if I intended publishing  
a Mundari dictionary, the Government would print that, the more so as the  
English of the MSS. I had just returned, was far from being up to the  
mark, In 1906 or 1905, Sir Androw Fraser, then Lieutenamt-Governor of  
pn tlie

1 asked the Rev. Fr. Peter Hipp 8.J., who has received an ¢xoellent  
musical training in Germany, to ac:ompany m2 to my former mission station  
in Sarwada, and try to fix, in European notation as far@as possible, the  
melodies of the Mundas. ,Then for some days there was musi¢ and singing «  
from morning till evening, and allthe dances were gone throngh before us  
even as they ate exccuted on the village dancing grounds. After a remarkably  
short time, Fr. Hipp, to the great astonis hment of the Mundas ‘and to their  
still greatew delight, played their dumad, their dul&i and their rabaga to  
perfection, After minute and careful obscrvations, the melodies and the  
drum-accompaniments were, in the course of many repetitions, fixed in notes,  
and Rev. Fr. Hipp put down the results of his observations! in a prefatory  
note, which together with ths music, app2ars in the article on Mundari  
musio, under the word, durag. In 1910 the Rev. Fr. Hug» Aman S.J, of  
the German Jesuit Mission in Bombay, an able musician and stadent of  
musical analysis, was staying for some months in Ranchi, I gave him the  
notes of Rev. Fr. Hippand persuaded him also to accompany me to Sarwada  
and give me his own judgment, after le had himsolf heard the songs and the  
music. The results of his observations are given together with those of  
Rev. Fr. Hipp.  
In the prosent stage of ethnology a very exact description of abso~  
lutely all the irpplements used by aboriginal races, of their dwollings and = °  
their mode of gonstructing them, is deemod indispensible for the purpose of  
ascertaining the so-called caltural circles t> which they belong. That is  
especially so in the case of the Mundas, who arc hardly represented at all ‘is  
in most European museums. Here the Rev. Fr. Louis Cardon S. J, has :  
rendered a signal service. In a set of very exact drawings made by  
himself, ho copicd all that the Mundas possess and uso in the shape of  
implements, furnitare and dwellings, and, by decomposing all these things  
and drawing all the details, he has offered to ethnologists advantages, which  
even the best musoums can hardly offer. It would have been too expensive  
to have them clichéed and insorted singly into ths des2riptive texts spread  
over the wholegrork. Therefore the well known Manich art-firm,  
Meisenbach Riffarth and Co. have arranged thom into tables or Plates, as  
I call them in the text, which are gathered into a separate fasciclo,  
In this, every object has printed unler it a serial numbar or letter  
and its Mundari name. This facilitates reference from the oxplanatory  
texts to the illustrations and vice-versa. Valuable a3 these illustrations  
aro, they ate not Fr. Cardon’s only contributions to the work. Being  
at present the senior Chota ‘Nagpur missionary and the one best  
acquainted with and most esteemed by the Aborigines, be is in position to

Among the many Mundas who have given information and explanations  
about the meanings and the idiomatic uses of words and about the economia  
social and religious laws and customs of their race, the following deserve to  
be specially mentioned, both on account of the value of their informations

Rufus Horo of Murbu; Sahadco Chutia Purti of Otongora, Daniel and his  
brother Joseph Horo of Lonkata, To all of them Rey. Fr, A. Vay  
Emelen §, J. and mysolf shall ever remain very grateful.

(9)  
And therefore I stiek to the terminology proposed there, which speaks of the  
funotions then and there performed by a word in a given sentence.  
In the game Introduction I have shown too that in Mundari there are  
no compound sentences with rslative and other subordinats clauses, "having  
subjects and predicates of their own, baing subordinated to a principal  
Proposition. The Mundari sentences is always a simple proposition, in which  
all onr subordinate clauses are expressed by participial forms, modified, when  
required, by affixes denoting tims, space, manner or condition.” All the  
functions thus performed are explained under the leading word-forms of  
these affixes as they oveur in the alphabetical order. *  
The other main characteristics of the language, such as the absence af  
relative and correlative pronouns, the absenc? of grammatical genders, the  
division of words with a substantive function into two classes, namely those  
denoting living beings and those denoting inanimate objects, the graannatical  
consequences of this, and finally, the inszrtion of diract an indirset pron>-  
minal objects into transitive predicates, are fully explained ian my Geammar.  
These explanations ary summarily repsated suo losa in tha course of thia  
work,

*The ceremonial foot-washings are  
called, hafa-adbuy. They have a reli-  
gious as well as a merely social  
character, It mast always be per-  
formed in front of the house. 1°  
Religious, A Munda returning fron  
abroad, where other castes or races  
are living, may not enter his bLou-e  
before his wife has washed bis fect.  
This washing is qonsicersd as a kind  
of baptism, which removes and con-  
dones any impurities against his own  
caste or &ili, which he may have  
unwittingly or accidentally contracted  
among the strangers: eta jatikoate  
ropa imta, kui bangaire, kora_  
hundamsie diraca airii rurajaked  
en'e kuyi kajue alugra, if at the  
moment of his reiura from among  
other castes the wife of a Munda Lé  
not at home, be must stay at the back  
of the house until she r‘torns, then  
the performs the ceremony of the  
foot-wasbing. 2° Social. This ablu- ,  
tion is du> as the most elementary  
mark of civility to any relative visiting  
the family. It oceara also in nearly  
every one of the numerous ccremonies,  
which precede marringe. The foot-  
washing is also somitimee offered to  
non-relatives as a mark of gratitude  
for some great fervice rendered to the  
family. It is annually offered to the  
village smith on the day of the  
vo called baraeili.

The adighongako are the object of  
the ancestor worship, The forms this  
worship has assumed among the  
Mundas ware deseribed fully in the  
articles under = laporoh, — badidirt,  
sasandivi and govsid, wherejit is shown  
how it pervades their whuly social  
organization, regulating — especially  
their right of inheritance. The sacri«  
ficial formulas show, that the Mundas  
believe them to enjoy the company of  
Singbonga and the tutelary sp'rits he  
has appointed; for they are invited  
together with these spirits and Sing-  
bénga to participate in all the feasts  
and the sacrifices offered by the  
community in the course of the year.  
Bat whatever forms the ancestor wor-  
ship may have assumed amongst  
different races and at different times,  
it is always a manifestation and a  
proof of that elementary and invin-  
cible belief in the immortality of the  
human soul, which demands a certain  
communication between the living:  
and the dead. We find it among the!  
most ancient surviving aborigines of  
the east, such as the Veddas, with  
whom the Mundas and their conge-  
is no doubt that it continued unin-  
terruptedly from those earlirst times,  
of the ancestors are believed to be  
in the company of Singbonga and  
the tutelary spirits, we find no trace  
of anything like a real deification in  
the Mundari forms of ancestor wor-  
ship; they are in the parom, i.e,  
beyond the reach of our senses (see  
article on Joga) but not above  
the nature (supra nataram) of the  
human spirit ia anything like the  
christion sense of the term superng-  
tural. They are supposed to remain  
spirits with meroly human aspirations,  
feelings and affections, keeping even  
their chasactoristic individual inclima-

with greon, terete, somewhat fleshy

*aisum syn, of umbu/, I. sbst.,  
shadow, the overshadowing. The  
Mundas share the wide-spread belief  
that special virtue attaches to the  
shadow of persons. So, v.g., at  
cock-fghts, a sport very much in  
favotr with them, the owners of two  
eocks about to fight each other, will  
try in an unostentations manner to  
move about in such a way as to  
throw their shadow over the cock of  
their antagonist. They beliove.that  
he who first succeeds in this, will  
thercby throw a gort of spell on that  
cock which deprives -if of its wits  
or makes its courage come to fail.  
Similarly they believe that the *  
hatuboxgako cause people to get  
sick, by overshadowing them: nj  
Tkirbowgaa aisumtee hasuakana, this  
man has breome sick by an over-  
shadowing of Iéirbouga. e  
Sicknoss may also be caused by  
the shadow of a villager touching  
or falling on one of these village  
spirits, This belief scems to rest  
on a difforent reason, The spirit  
is believed to be irritated by the  
touch of such a shadow, and there-  
fore causes illness in order to force  
the delinquent to make reparation  
by a sacrifice. The village spirits,  
being the spirits of the Asur women,  
are not of the same caste ‘as the  
Mundas, and consider themselves  
therefore as in some way defiled by  
a Munda’s shadow, even as a brah-  
min considers himself defiled by the  
shadow of a pariah. This gains  
; support from the fact that the  
oralongako, the honse spirits, who  
are of the Munda race, are neither  
offended by being overshadowed by  
Mundas, nor hurt Mundas Ly  
overshadowing them: orgbonga kae  
eitumkoa, mendo Ikirbonga, Bura-  
bornga emanko, house spirits do not  
hurt by overshadowing, but (only)  
Tkirborga, Buruborga and others of  
the same class. This, therefore is  
a specifically Hindu belief. Whe-  
ther the wider belief in the virtue  
attaching to human shadows be  
also taken from the Hindus or wag

The ent. prst. implies that an action  
already begun, must bo continued

circumstances of posilivo orlers

second case the continuity is only a

rales their whole social and political

fully calealatol to preclude every-

when in the couree of tims, under

ingship, they rigoaronsly excluded

feronce the internal arrange-  
ments of their village communities  
ani their district or mankipati orga-  
nization, as is shown in the articles

To render intercourse pleasant it is

in

The only civil organization in the  
world, which has with some success  
resisted state olaims as far as they  
are unjustifiable and opposed to  
family claims, is the Indian caste  
system, which is but an Aryan modi-  
fication of the tribal organization of  
the aboriginal races of India, as I  
show conclusively, I trust, in t  
article on afi» Its worst abuses  
have arisen directly out of that over-  
Jord system which the Aryans  
brought along with them to India  
and which they grafted on to the  
old tribal organization.

*antar (Sk. anfar, distance ; Or.  
atri) 1. sbst. The Mundas do not  
plough in straight lines from end to  
end, turning the plough at the end  
of the first furrow so that the scoond  
comes to lie immediately against the  
first, and the third against the  
sceond and so on, so that the whole

as ig done in Europe. They divide,  
by conjecture, the whole ficld into  
sections, each of which they intend *  
finishing in one stroke by turning:  
round the whole of it in constantly  
diminishing elliptic lines until the  
Dullooks ean no longer find the re-  
quired space for completing a new  
ellyptic inner furrow. Such a sec-  
tion they call mod antar. The  
seoond section is averaged so that it  
includes the unploughed part of the  
first section, and the third, so as to  
include the unplonghed part of the  
the second, and s0 on. When the  
unploughed part of the first section  
is ploughed up with the turning of  
the second antar, then they say that  
the first andar lomjana, is completely  
ploughed. The whole process is  
described as follows by » Manda:  
Nekageko sila : sida moiad antaroa ;  
en aniardo sihurirajanci, urjko  
Diuren kako ituia, ente org miadgko  
antaréa, sidauterre lomjanci orggeko  
antaréa, en tatomrg lomjante orgko  
antarta; enlekate janad baria

I. trs. and intrs. This term, its  
synonyms and contraries as well as  
certain words and phrases generally  
connected with them, refer partly  
to the correct slant of the plough-  
share to the bottom line of the  
plough, and partly to the correct  
slant of the ploughshaft to the  
ground line.” It is very difficult for  
a foreigner to use this whole com-  
plex of words correctly, and  
sometimes even to understand them  
without the following preliminary  
explanations :  
In an ndze (Pl, XXXIX, fig, 5),

angar  
; angar ‘  
a kurpi (same Pl. fig. 7),and a boe  
(Pl XUI, fig. 7), the angle at  
which their blade stands to the  
handle, is determined by the depth  
they are intended to reach when  
cutting or boeing. The average  
angle required in each, is known Ly  
experience and arranged accordingly  
by the makers. Any appreciable  
deviation from this angle renilers  
them more or less unfit for the  
purpose.  
The same holds good of the plongh  
with this difference, that here there  
are two distinet angles to be attended  
to, viz., the obtuse angle at which  
the share is inserted into the plough  
sand the acute angle formed by the  
ploughshaft and the ground line. Each  
of these angles must have an average,  
determined by the depth of the  
furrow. Any appreciable Aeviation  
from these averages will affect the  
depth of the furrow. It will either  
be too deep for the cattle to draw  
the plough easily, oF not deep  
enough to benefit the seed.  
The Mundas use ¢ number of terms  
denoting* the deviations from the  
correct averages. Some of these  
apply only to the angle between the  
plough and ite share, others only to  
the angle between the shaft and the  
ground line. To increase the  
difficulty, the Nagori dialect has one  
set of terms, and the Hasada dialect  
has another, and the Keramundas  
around Ranchi have a third one.  
The whole plough ready for  
work is shown on Pi. XII,  
fig, 1; A shows guly the  
plough, xaéa/, B shows a section  
through it, C a section throngh the  
opening into which the share is  
inserted ; fig. 2 shows the share  
itself, which, when inserted, must  
protrude beyond the ploughf as  
shown in fig. 1. If the share were  
inserted so that its protruding part  
would form no angle with the  
bottom line of the nagal, it would  
not ent into the ground at all, It  
must "be inserted pointing down-  
wards so as to form the correct angle  
with the bottom line. If thie slant  
be very slight, the angle will be  
very obtuse, and the angle dcoreasea  
in proportion as the slant gets more  
and more pronounced,  
But the sharper this slant or the  
smaller the angle, the deeper does the  
plough cut, and the less the slant,  
or the greater the anglo, the lesa  
deep will be the furrow made  
by the plough with tie consequences  
stated above. There is only one  
correct slant .and only one correct  
angle, If this average be deviated  
from by making the angle too obtuse  
so that the furrow is not deep  
enough, both the Nag. and the Has.  
dialects denote this defect by the  
word kimox, (in Nag, also seo).  
Hence this term means, to put the  
share at snch a slant that it does  
not cut deep enough, to give the  
share too little slant : pé/em homon-  
feda ; in the p. v.: pal homonakana,  
the share has too little slant. The  
contrary of this term is daraé, to  
give the share such asharp slant that  
it cuts too deep + palem daratheda s

Here then the raco’s healthy ins-*  
tinct has rigorously barred out the  
dangers of the disgraceful and humi-

most eanctuary of the family,  
Oceasion not only makes thieves,  
it also makes sinners of other kinds  
and especially delinquents against  
morality. That is why the always”  
eminently practical wisdom of the  
Munda race bas arranged the bas-°  
tions of its prophylactic customs in  
such a manner as to exclude ocea-  
sions, and at the same time act as  
a constant reminder. The reproach  
of prudery will of course be made  
against most of these rules, espe-  
cially by those for whom a certain  
amount of that kind of prndery  
would fit very well.  
It is difficult for us Europeans to  
understand the rigour with which  
these and other rules about mere  
externalities are obeyed, as well as  
the apparently superstitions dread  
and horror with which their trans-  
gression is regarded, a8 though they  
were most heinous crimes in them-  
selves. So far as 1 could ace, no

particular means are used to obtain

@ natural instinct. A couple of  
examples will show this: During  
the sixteen years I lived among the  
Aborigines, I heard only of ono

rent missions and bad shortly before

tianity altogether. The German  
+Evangelical missionaries were the  
first to enter the Munda country in  
1845 and since they very naturally  
and rightly assisted the Aborigines  
against the oppressing alien intru-  
ders, with advice and intercession,  
* they gradually gained many thou-  
sands of adherents. They insisted  
with ‘more energy than wisdom on  
substituting the fashion of shaking  
hands for the Mundari manner of  
saluting, and on several other Euro-  
pean oustoms which have really  
nothing to do with Christianity.  
Later on in the Anglican and  
Catholic chapels too mats, forming  
part of the chapel furniture, were

divine service, Neither Latheran,  
nor Anglican, nor Catholic mis-  
sionaries seem ever to have sus-  
pected bow unreasonably and how

of their flocks by these two practices, .  
and they complained that it was  
so difficult to get the women to  
attend Sunday service. Meanwhile  
the pagan part of the population  
expressed their horror at the trans-  
gression of most sacred obligations  
thus imposed by Christian mission-  
aries. “Since this new religion,  
they would say, has come into our  
country these Christians commit sins,  
which were never heard of in former  
times’. And, as wag to be expeot-  
ed, they considered all the evils of  
the time as punishments for the sins  
of the Christians, This and other  
facts show how regrettable it is that  
modern European missionaries find  
it so difficult to ril themselves of  
the rather arrogant conviction that  
everything European is and must be  
much better than everything else ia  
the world, which makes them con-  
demn a limine as pagan and there-  
fore as bad, customs they neither  
know nor care to enquire into.  
One more example to show  
how these anciont racial prohibitions  
become as it were natural instincts :  
Once on a journey from the Chakra-  
dharpur railway statior up to the  
Chota Nagpur plateau I stopped  
at noon in the foreat under a fine old  
tree and shared my mid-day meal  
with my groom and my carrier. It  
consisted in what I had bought at  
the railway station, fresh bread,  
a great delicacy for me, and  
a Bologaa sausage, a atill greater  
delicacy for my two guests, for the  
Mundas are great fanciers of pork,

Towards the end of our meal I asked  
in the ourse of conversation  
whether they knew what kind of  
meat they were eating. As they  
answered unbesitatingly that it was  
pork, I told them that it was  
generally aaid that horseflwh was  
mixed with it. I never thought for  
the moment that my groom  
belonged to aw sept forbidden  
to eat horsellesh because the horse  
was their sept totom. Immediately  
ihe poor felloygturned ash-grey and,  
with an anxious loadk at me, asked :  
Sir is that really true? As soon  
as Thad said that I thought so, he  
yan aside and vomikd his whole  
meal. Ilo was neither unwell nor  
bad he eaten too much tall. The  
sole fact of his getting aware that  
he had eaten, unsuspectingly, the  
forbidden totem flesh, produced in  
him that violent nausea.  
When in this and other articles  
I deliberately maintain, that the  
principles which rule the moral and  
economic life of the Mundas, are  
superior in solid practical wisdom to  
those which rule the moral and eto-  
namic life of modern Europe, I only  
mean to say, that mere intellectual  
progress is neither proof of, nor  
guarantee for a corresponding moral  
progress, even as mere mechanical  
skill with the euperabundance of its  
products is, by itsclf alone, no proof  
of economic wisdom and equity, nor  
a guarantee for social concord and  
happiness. It is self-evident that  
the complicated life ‘of modern  
Burope raises a number of problems  
which are not even suspected" by a  
simple agricnitural people like the  
Mundas, ‘But it is equally evident,  
that mere intellectual and material  
Progress neitlcr necessitates nor  
justifies the throwing aside of those  
moral restraints without which, as a  
matter of fact, neither the family  
nor the state nor the tage can remain  
long in moral health afd vigour.  
It would of course be absurd to  
pretend that the youth of the Munda  
race is free from the dangers and  
lapses, which are the common lot of  
all youth between the years of  
puberty and marriage, But the  
Mundas enjoy the advantago of  
having that dangerous period short~  
ened by an carly marriage, and of  
being deterred from the worst ex-  
cesses by the rigour of the above  
sanctions. If, in the last sixty  
years or so, there has been a relaxa-  
tion in public morality, then that is  
much less the fault of the Mundas *  
than of those who have, in all kinds of  
ways, provoked them to cvery form  
of vice for the sake of filthy Iucre, at  
atime, when the whole aocial orga-  
nization of the race was weakened  
and, to a great extent, destroyed by  
outward interferences over which  
the Mundas themselves had no con-  
trol whatsoever. (See the articles  
onder arkali, arandi, bhuinari,  
caitkidar, dike and others).

about the unavoidable accidents of  
life? Rather take the day as it  
comes with the dotica it imposes

Poor lives the pure joys of a healthy

api-kuli I. sbst., a leaf cop made  
of three leaves pinned together so as  
to be triangular in shape.  
*Such cups are required in saori+  
ficial rites, in which the number  
three playsan important part, It  
is not only the number three, but  
uncven numbers in general which  
play a part in sacrificial rites and  
in divination: Thus, in sacrifiocs  
either free or five little heaps of  
rice are pot onthe ground and on  
each some of the blood of the sacri-  
fieed animal is allowed to drip,  
When they go to consult the omens  
before a marriage, they go in groups  
of 3,5, 7 or9, To most onquirics  
about the reason of this, one gete  
the usual answer thay the ancestors  
have always done so, One however  
gave the following reason: Sing-  
bonga has made all things in pairs,  
so that an uneven number implies  
an imperfection. Hence in sacri-  
fiecs and divinations, in all of which  
men want or ask something from  
Singbonga, they use these numbers  
as an acknowledgment of a nced, an  
imperfection, and a symbol that  
now they apply to Singbonga that  
he may ect things aright by supply-  
ing what is needed. This com-  
pletion by Singbonga of uneven  
into even numbers, is therefore  
equivalent to asking Singbonga to  
make whole or healthy what is ill,  
to remedy what is amiss in any way,  
to grant the help which one expects  
from him in any particular need,

The Mundas are firm believers in  
the survival, after death, of the  
umbul or ji, the shade or spirit.  
Moreover they hold that death does  
not even snap the family ties. The  
spirits of tha deceased remain in  
the adin, the store-room of the  
house, continue their interest in the  
family and show it as orgbongako,  
guardian spirits of the house. As,  
such they claim a certain worship,  
ie, honour and sacrifices. The  
now prevailing religious system hag  
delegated to the pshan the ancestor

This fact screens the malpractices

against the intervention of the

To safeguard the liberty of emi-  
grant labourers, a law had been  
enacted, which ordered every emi-+  
grant recruited by arkatis, to be  
placed before an English Magistrate,  
previous to his leaving the country.  
There he was asked whether he  
knew that he was on his way to the  
Assam tea gardens, and whether he  
went there of his own free will, In  
Assam a similar examination took  
place before they put their thumb-  
mark to the contract by which they  
obliged themselves to work in o  
particular garden for a period of  
5 years, But this very law was, by  
the shrewd malice of the arkatis,  
turned against their victims on  
account of the incredible gullibility  
of the latter. Those repeated offi-  
cial examinations, coupled with the  
home again, were infallibly caught  
and then, for a third time, placed  
before a magistrate, who, this time  
condemued them to jail for} breach  
of contract, gave an official appear-  
ance to the whole business in the  
eyes of the Mundas. The’ constant  
assertions of the arkatis, that the  
Assam tea gardens were a Govorn-  
ment coneern and they themsdlyea  
were Government officials, clinched  
the matter. Did they not wear  
high pugrees and chaprasses (largo  
oval badges) like the court peorts,  
called chaprassis? Great were the  
powers of the olaprassi: he came to  
the villages backed by a policeman,  
to attach crops and cattle in exeeu-  
tion of court dcerces, ho brought  
the summons to appear in court, and  
any one who dared to disobey a  
summogs of his, was inevitably led  
off as a prisoner by the ‘irresistible  
policeman. Ina word, the chaprassi  
was a terror only a little inferior  
to the almighty daroga (the native  
subinspector of police). I had  
beard myself this belief expressed  
before was long in the country,  
and I had langhed at it, never  
suspecting, how deeply it was rooted  
and how dangerous it was, until I  
witnessed the first famine in Chota  
Nagpur. Since the people appeared  
unwilling to take famine loans from  
the Government officials, the Govern-  
ment offered money to the mission«  
aries, asking them to give it to the  
people as loans, I had received

In the economic life of the Mundas,  
weekly markets play a much greater  
part than they do in that of other  
Indians or Europeans, because abori-  
ginal villages have no shops of any  
kind, so that everything needed in  
the household must be got from the  
nearest local markets. The distance  
between these varies from 6 or 7 to  
at most 15 or 16 miles. In or quite  
close to most of them there isa  
licensed liquor shop, Each market  
has a fixed day in the week so that

out of proportion to the business  
transacted in them. Most of this is  
done by the married women. The  
elder men come to take their drop at  
the liquor shop and to discuss the  
Politics of the district; matrons

da girl coming to the tea gardens.

To obtain that result, they proceed

until the distention caused by this  
increasing bundle of necdles, has

nearly so. Then it is replaced by  
aroll of very thinly split bamboo,  
whi-h, by its natural tension, keeps  
the opening for some time at the  
desired size. This roll is painted  
abright red, soas to Le already  
a kind of ornament by itself, After  
some time it is zemoved and replaced  
by the Hindu pendant, called taradi  
by the Mundas, All these inserted

*arki (Sink. and T. arakku, spirits,  
from arakke, to grind; Sad. arki ;  
U. arg; Or, arkhi) sbst., mabua  
brandy: arkikgo duljda, or arkigo  
cnajada, they distil mahua brandy,  
This is distilled from the blossom of  
the mandukamdarn, Bassia latifolia,  
Willd,; Sapotaceac, The distillttion  
is generally done by Mahomedans,  
under Government inspection, and  
the sale of the liquor is subjcet to  
a pretty heavy tax, heavy enough  
to yield a good yearly revenue, but  
not heavy enough to eflicaciously  
combat drunkenness, Distillerics  
and liquor shops are spread over the  
whole country, at intervals averag-  
ing about 10 miles or less. Exyecial-  
ly on market-Jays the liquor is  
carried into the remotest hamlets of  
the country. Although the law  
prescribes that liquor shops may not  
be on the markct-place itself, they  
are so close that these frequently  
leeome the scenes of revolting  
drunkenness on market-days. A  
certain number of men go to the  
market only to get intoxicited. A  
moderate acquaintance with the  
country and the people sullices to  
show the moral and physical ravages  
eaused by these liquor shops. When  
the Mundas get drunk on their  
home-brewed rice-becr, they aban-  
don themselves toakind of gond-  
humoured mirth. But drunkenness  
from mahua brandy renders them  
quarrelsome and offensive. I have  
been told by Mundas that they  
themselves have remarked that  
people living in the vicinity of liquor  
shops get emaciated, or, dried up, as  
they put it, if they drink that stuff  
habitually, It is doubtful whether  
the Mondas knew the process of

' Intoxicants are a dangerous temp-  
tation to all races, The supply of  
food-grains out of which the abori-  
ginal races used to brew their beers,  
wasasa rule, so limited, that it  
allowed over-indulgence only during  
avery short time immediately after  
the harvest, and that only there  
where agriculture was advanced far  
enough to allow a regular harvest  
at ull. They had therefore but  
little occasion to observe the disas-  
trons effects of the long-continued  
habit of drunkenness on distilled  
liquors, because of these they had  
no knowledge. When therofore the  
obnoxious cheap brandies came on  
them with the suddenness of a flood,  
sAhey had no longer any time to  
" work out any prophylactio measvres  
"gainst this vice, as they had dono  
so successfully against social injus-  
tiees and against sexual immorality.  
It is an incontestable fact, that low-  
minded and unscrupulous European  
merchants have hastened on the  
extinction of not a few newly-disco-  
vered tribes and rages by offering  
them unlimited quantities of cheap  
brandica in exchange for frequently  
very valuable wares. This has been  
especially the case where the im-  
morality of the same class of Euro-  
peans destroyed the high moral  
purity which had, till then, prevailed  
among those unwary people. Thus  
the valuable ethnological and philo-  
logical information,the closo study of  
those Aborigines would have given  
us, has been irretrievably lost.  
It is certainly greatly to the credit  
of the Aborigines of Chota Nagpur  
that they answered so gencrously  
to the temperance ecimpaign orga-  
nized with the establishment of the  
Catholic Co-operative Credit Soci-  
ety of ChotaNagpur. Thereby they  
proved, that they still retain mueb  
of that healthy social wisdom which  
lias created those economic, social  
and moral customs, to which alone  
they owe theit survival in spite of  
the constant hardships and perse-  
cutions from others.

Formerly, when the Mundas intend  
ed breeding silk-worms on a certain  
spot in the forest, they would cat  
all the brashwood and arrange it  
Tight round the spot by way of an  
enclosure. Sach a place would be  
called lumamara, the enclosure of  
a breeding ground for silk-worms,  
and the same: phrase, ured trsly.,  
meant, to prepare a breading ground  
for silk-worms. In the few places  
where this practice is still in vogue,  
(Nag), they say, v. g., /umanhole  
agabiurkedkoa, we have olosed in the  
breeding ground of the silk-worms,  
But though the enclosure is now no  
longer used in most parts, the phrase  
remains and is applied to (1) the  
breeding ground of — silk-worms; ”  
lumamaratg aminepe. (2) the breed~  
ing of silk-worms: /umamaratle  
purg pads vamoa.

*N. B, (1) The following very  
strange superstition. The Mundas  
believe that the silk-worms would be  
harmed if one of the following  
words were pronounced on the breed-  
ing ground: mandi, cooked rice,  
kaii, a crow, ca/w, an earthen water-*  
pot, fumbult, a wasp, oboreéré, the,  
Indian cuckoo, sakamnada,a mantis.  
Hence», when spoaki on the

These substitutes have no meaning

nd that the Mundas believo that

the’ men of that country. Kagkela  
is derived from the Sad. &inkata  
and handupg means a gaper.  
(2) the saying: gara ¢i ara, abbre-

To dam up a river or to breed silk-  
worms are entreprises in which one  
eis never sure of success. Hence the  
phrase is used of entreprises in  
which success is doubtful: it is like  
drawing an embankment across  
a river or like breeding silk-worms,  
Tnstéad of this, they also use the  
phrase, gora ci ara, it is like sowing  
paddyon a high ground, or like  
breeding silk-worms.

* The Mundas have no real horti-  
culture. Many of their huts have  
indeed a small hedged-in plot, a  
dakri, at the back, but the little  
cultivation that takes place thera,  
does not deserve the name of horti-  
cultare. Although all European  
vegetables grow at least as well in  
Chota Nagpur as in Europe during’  
the winter months if properly  
watered, the Mundas have, up to  
date, not yet taken to their culti«  
vation, because thcir poverty does,

arg

asarule, not allow them to nk  
the necessary wells nor to provide  
effective hedges to protect the  
vegitables against fow] and especi-  
ally nguinst goats. The chase,  
even in those parts where there is  
some forest left, yields no longer  
anything worth mentioning. The  
annual general hunts are kept up  
only because they are surrounded  
with the halo of a primitive custom  
and because it offers some satis-  
faction to the lunting passion  
which has not yet died out in the  
race. Nor can they afford fo slavght-  
er cattle, goats, pigs and sherp  
to provide anything like a regular  
meat diet. The only meat they  
get, ic that of decrepid old buffaloes  
and bullocks dying of exhaustion  
or discase, and the flesh of the  
fowls, goats or p'gs they are obliged  
to sacrifice either Ly ancient customs  
or by the order of the derd, the  
professional witch findkr. They dig  
no tanks, like the indus, and  
rivers, rich in fish, are few and far  
between. ‘True, they are passionate-  
ly fond of fish, and their dexterity  
in knitting nets and making fish  
traps of all descriptions, seems to  
show, that at some former time they  
must have lived in }1.ces where fish  
@bounded. But the limited quan-  
tities of small fry they draw from  
the streamlets of their hilly  
country and from their flooded  
rice-fields cannot be reckoned among  
their stample food ; they ar2 hardly  
Sufficient to offer an occasional  
addition to their scanty daily meals.  
The worst of all is that there are very  
few Mundas now adays, having  
enough of good rice-fields left, to  
supply them with a dceo.nt meal of  
cooked rice for every day of the  
year. A large proportion of families  
cannot even reap enough on their  
wretched patches, to give ewry  
member a meal of rice for two  
months.  
On what then do these poor people  
subsist ? That was the qustion I  
very often asked myself, when I had  
got a char insight into their real  
state of destitution. This made me  
conceive the plan of getting to-  
gether a complete list of all the  
wild plants of which they eat the  
leaves or flowers or roots and tubers.  
I have already stuted that Rev. Fr. *  
Cardon, during one of the Jate  
famines, bad made a list of some 80  
such plants. Rev. Fr, Van Emelen  
has not only completed that list,  
but also added {to nearly all the  
names the botanical terms, so that  
any ethoologit, intending to follow  
up this matter, may now compare  
the plants eaten by the Mundas,  
with those consumed by other abori-  
ginal races, and also cowpare the  
nemes given to them by the Mun-  
das, with those used by other  
races. Very probably such a com-  
parison will be of advantage to  
comparative philology as well as to  
ethnology in- general. Further-  
more, the list throws a new light  
on the means of subsistence, primi-  
tive man found in the abundance  
of food offcred to him by nature

N. B. the idioms in which the words  
atar, duray and goali stand as d.o.  
toarg:  
*1° In dances it is oustomary that  
| at the end of any particular danoa  
the leading girl in the line, i.e, the  
one to the left side, yields up her e  
Place and goes to the last place, i.e.,

leading place, the a&ar, during one  
danee. This rotiriog from the lead-  
ing place is called, atar arg or  
ater argn: ne kuri janad agar kae  
argéa, this girl never gives up the *  
first place.  
* 2° When boys and girls of several

It is this giving up the dance to  
the boys of another villoge after the  
third dance, which is called dwray  
arg, ltly., to give up the ringing,  
because in the dances it is the boys  
who take the lead in the songs.  
*3° There are two different plough-  
ing contracts in use among the  
Mundas: (1) those who have no  
ploughing cattle, are allowed to  
plough theirown land for one day  
with the cattle of another man, if  
they first plough the owner's land  
for two days. (2) Those who have bat  
one bullock or buflulo, get the loan  
of a second bullock or buffalo from  
ancighbour,and may, then plongh  
their own And for two days provid-  
ed they plough the land of the neigh-  
bour for one day. It is this  
yielding up of either a yoke of oxen  
(or buffalves) or a single bullock or  
buffalo to another for jloug'sing his  
fields for one or two days which is  
denoted by the phrase, gou/i «rg. It  
may be used with or without ind., 0.  
goali argata or goali argéa.  
The same phrase is also used with  
the meaning, to give others also an  
opportunity of joining in a conversa~  
tion or of putting in a word now  
and again: miad batabata burialo  
pitiatele hijylena, anjdo goali kae  
argéa.  
arabura (Sad. larlardek; 4H.

only carpenter’s implements known

arandl  
every Munda is his own.carpenter,

properly on the round ridge-pole.

- * * The monogamy of the Mundas  
and other aboriginal races is one  
of the most interesting ethnological  
facts: Up to the appearance of  
Edward Westermarck’s History of  
Maman Marriage in 18), universal  
accepted — it a  
scientifically established fact, that  
monogany was one of the highest and  
therefore last stages in human evolu-  
tion. Since a closer observation af the

former _ seientifizally — establishod  
fuct had to be remodelled so as to

gamy and this ont of promiscuity,

course the natural state of primitive

monogamous, This new thé@y is  
as simple and as plansible as that

In Westermarck’s firsé ehapter

evolutioniste as  
Brandi  
among the Chelonia, that with

mitted to man from some aye-like

The real reason of all this vagae-

that questions regarding the inner  
nature of the intellect and free will,  
lie beyond the reach of the so-called  
natural sciences and can be settled

consciousness.  
Consciousness tells us that our  
sensations, i.e, all the modes of  
knowledge which we acquire through  
thosa bodily organs we have in  
common with the lower animals,  
are indissolably connected with  
space and time, because they inva-  
riably present themselves as quali-  
ties of something extended: The  
eye soes only coloured things of  
some shapo or another, we hear  
things sounding for a shorter or

In the rules or laws I now subjoin,  
the Mundas have preserved a social  
creat’o of prehistor'c times, full of

writing in our Mission school. The

In order to understand them fully

must bear in mind, that these rega-  
lations were mide in those far-away  
times, in which there was no trace  
as yet of a state organization, stand-  
ing outside of, and above the family  
organization, which could have  
efficiently protected individual rights  
and settled = interfamilial and  
intertribal disputes. The family  
was the ohly institution able  
to offer tothe individual all that  
help and protection he stands so  
absolutely in need of, and which  
consequently enjoyed all those rights  
over the individual, without which  
an efficacious protection would be  
* impossible. By the word family  
I here mean that particular organi-  
zation, which the Mundas have  
preserved up tothe present day in  
afew typical, unbroken Kuntkatti  
villages that have survived the  
whplesale dostruction, In such  
———_—  
villages all the men are descendants  
of the original founder of the  
village, so that all of them are close  
blood-relations. These men together  
with their wives an! children con-  
stitute the village family or commu-  
nity. The term indintdual family,  
denotes the usual monogamous couple  
with their legitimate children. Ille-  
gilimate children can never parti-  
cipate in the rights enjoyed by the  
members of either the individuil or  
the village family.  
The source of the family’s authori-  
ty over the individual lies in our very  
nature which inspires the individual  
with a vivid consciousness of his  
| absolute dependence on the family  
{especially in pre-state times) and,  
at the same time fills him with  
affections, the strength and trnler-  
ness of which mitigate considerably  
that which is unpalatable in the  
senee of dependence. It is this  
which makes the family authority  
not only prior in time but also prior  
to, in right or nature, and stronger  
than any state authority, so that  
any encroachment of the latter on  
the ground of the former always  
amounts to a violation of human  
nature, detrimental and sometimes  
disastrous to social happiness. It  
is but natural that the family should  
instinctively have sought to defend  
its authority especially against the  
stronger individual instincts, such  
as come into play in the marriage  
question, which, if left uncurbed,  
would so easily lead to selfish revolt _  
against that authority and thence

the few unsuspecting European  
officers were wondering why the  
Mundas were so dissatisfied !

was a mouthful of cooked rice,  
a leaf-capful of stale beer, a piece of  
turmeric, a small wooden pyxful of  
red lead, a small earthen vessel  
full of oil for anointing.  
The main burden of this story is  
the fuct that in the beginning tha  
groom and bride had a real right  
of choice, whereas the parents and  
relatives exercised only a kind of  
veto-right, and that the passions of  
the young husbands ondangored  
the stability of marriago to such an

has a historical foandation, whereas  
the suggvetion, that the marriage

merely one of the many which the  
Mundas 60 readily invent to explain  
all kinde of historival fucts, They  
explain the origin of their many

evidently mere fictions. Even the  
appearance of the first uropeans

to a story which deserhos how

the cold, and how, now at last, some  
of them found their way back to  
their original home.  
The absence of puberty ceremonice  
among the Mundas is a noteworthy  
fact, Eqnally noteworthy is the fact,  
that there dors nut exist among them  
any of the beliefs or superstitions  
found among several other aboriginal  
races, forbidding marital intercourse  
during the pregnancy and even dur-  
| ing the nursing period of the mother.  
Close enyuirics have elicited the  
unvarying answer that the inter-  
course continues during pregnancy

it seems hurtful to the fetus, and  
that it is generally resumed some  
three wecks after the birth of  
a child, exceyit for special reasons, FS  
now and then. Delivery is gene-  
rally very easy and causes s0 little  
inconvenience, that christian mothys  
frequently bring their new-born  
babes themselves for baptism two  
or three days after their delivery.  
Finally another point worthy of  
notice is the fact, that the con:uia-

and festivities are finished and the  
marriage guests arc all dismiss.  
with due prosents, The festivities

third day the’ guests are dismissed.  
On the evening of that day the  
inmates of the groom’s house go and  
sloop elsewhere, leaving the whole  
house to the newly warricd couple  
for the consummation of marriage.

*Asur, Asur Manga sbst., an Asur.  
The Asors are a rapidly disappearing  
branch of some aboriginal race,  
represented now only by small  
groups of a few familics spread  
through the hills and forests of  
Sarguja, Jashpur, the Daltonganj  
district and here and there in the  
Ranchi district, Those living on  
a so-called pat (height) in the  
Barway did still, in 1895, when  
I met them, speak a Mundari dialect  
deviating less from the ordinary  
Mundari than that of the Korwae.  
That this whole tract was formerly  
occupied by Mundas, is shown by  
the names of the villages as well as  
by the burial stones met everywhere.  
But they were, like in some other  
parts of the Ranchi district, crowded  
out by the Oraons, and the few  
isclated groups of Mundas, found  
still hera and there, have now given  
up their own language to such an  
extent that after 1900 hardly a  
single couple of old people could be  
found who still knew Mundari,  
whereas the Barway Asurs, like the  
Korwas, adhered still partly at least,  
Asur  
to their Mundari dialects. They  
are, however, in every respeob far  
behind the Mandar, standing, it  
appears, closer to the Birhors tin  
their primitive civilization than to  
the Mundas. Like these, they are  
subdivided into Tiania Aaurs, i.e.,  
such as are settled in small villages,  
and Sieka or Uthiw Asurs, ie,  
such as retain the nomadic mode of  
life. The settled Asuras of the  
Ranchi district resent it when the  
Mundas call them Asur Baravko,  
Asur blacksmiths; they want to be  
called Asur Munduko. Some of  
those who live on the Barway pals  
practise iron smelting, but do not  
act as blacksmiths, probably because  
they consider that as against their ,  
caste. About their social and reli:  
gious customs hardly anything very  
definite is known as yet. Sarat  
Chandra Roy, author of 7he Mundas  
and Their Country, and Secretary for  
Anthropology and Folk-lore to the  
Bihar and Orissa Research Society,  
is the first who succeeded in eliciting  
from them information about their  
totemistio observances. Sce Vol.  
III, Part IV, page 567 (December  
Number of 1917). What he de-  
scribes ther, is in one respect at least,  
very different from the present  
practice of the Mundas in this  
matter. It may be summed up as  
follows =  
In an introductory paragraph he  
divides the Asurs into 3,classes, the  
Thania or settled, the Sdeka or]  
nomadic and the Birjia Aaurs, the  
latter being subdivided into

## Scan page 270

235  
Asur  
Asur  
Findéréhaé Birjiaa (such as” use  
Sindur ot their marriages), Telia  
Birjias (such as use only oil),  
Dudh Birjias (such as do not eat  
Deef) and Rarh Birjias (such as  
cat beef). In a foot-note he writes:  
“The Birjias are, however, to all  
intents ani purposes, ® separate  
tribe.” But most of his inform-  
ation bas been got from these Birjias.  
I. He gives the names of 22 birds,  
2 snakes, 1 fish and 9 animals as  
totem names, (Nearly all of these  
are Mundari words, and are also  
sipt names among the Mundas).  
The flesh of these is taboo to the  
respective septs, and intermarriage  
between members of the same sept  
is forbidden, However it is allowed  
* ,among members belonging to diffe-  
rent subdivisions of the same sept  
(even as it is among the Mundas).  
II. Among som: the sept totem  
is changed after 3 or 4 generations, *  
and with it the waa or ancestor  
spirit is also changed, and conse-  
quently the marriage Lar with other  
members of that family is also  
removed.  
ILL. In addition to the sept totem  
the Birjia Asurs have an individual  
totem the origin of which they  
explain as follows: The spirit of  
some relative or neighbour, living  
or deccused, overshadows a child  
at its Lirth, thus giving the ebild  
his own totem. The name of the  
overshadowing spirit is revealed  
by the mali, witch-finder, at the  
name-giving ceremony. In addition  
to the flesh of bis sept totem and  
his personal totem, the flush of the  
individual totem of his parents,  
children and wife is taboo for such  
an individnal.  
IV. The Birjia regards his totom  
as his kin. Hence whenever he  
mects au individual of that specics,  
he is obliged to salute it with an  
obeisance ; if he finds a dead indivi-  
dual of the species, he is obliged  
to weep over it, Lury or cremate  
it and Jeave the spot with an obei-  
sance,  
For women the flesh of their  
father’s totem is taboo, but not that  
of their hosbands,  
V. Some Birjias have no indivi-  
dual fotems. Others have forgotten  
the namo of their sept totem. But  
in spite of that they pretend that  
intermarriage between mombers of  
that sopt is forbidden. In such  
a state of things such marriages  
are likely to oveur, lence, when  
the wife gets frequently ill after the  
martiage, that is considered as ao  
sign that both husband and wife  
belong to the same sept, and there-  
fore divorce becomes obligatory.  
For this separation they still have  
the Mundari term, sakamearj, the  
tearing of the leaf.  
VI. Others while still consi-  
dering the flesh of the sept totem  
as taboo, allow the intermarriaga  
between members of the same sept,  
From these totemistic heresies,  
(if I may so call them,) the author  
concludes that totcmism is fast  
decaying among the Birjiss and

## Scan page 271

Asur  
other ttibes in these parts. It is  
evident, that in a tribe so rapidly  
dcereasing, the difficulty of finding  
wives of other septs, makes it im-  
yorsible to observe a rigid exoga-  
mous law.  
The author states that, when one  
of the men began giving informa-  
tion about the change of the sept  
totom after 3 or 4 generations, he  
was stopped by another man, und  
that after that, no further informa-  
tion could be obtained.

which it is expressed, would seem

natural enough that they should

form, As a matter of fact their  
present belief on the subject con-

They participate in the sacrifices of-  
fered by him to the local tutelary  
spirits, but they are excluded from  
the particular sacrifices offered to  
the village ancestors. The deseen-  
dants of such adopted settlers may  
ask and obtain leave from the village  
to placa a sasandiré (burial slab)  
over the graye of the persons thus  
adopted as a mark and ovidence of  
their khuntkutli rights. For this  
permission they are obliged to give  
a great dinner to the whole village.  
Aevond dinner is to be given by  
thom for the assistance offered at the  
actual bringing in and placing of |  
the sesandirt, just.as it is done by  
the original Khuntkattidars for such  
aservice, If the factof the adop-  
tion happen to practically fall into  
oblivion, the descendants silently  
pass iuto the sept of the adopting  
village. But this is dificult on  
account of their exclusion from the  
sacrifices to the ancestors.  
This practice is now restricted to  
the “Mankipati area, in which the  
ancient communal system still sur-  
vives in its main outlines. Adop-  
tions of the kind are a free gift  
which can be made only with the  
unanimous consent of the whole  
village family. It can neither be  
bought nor even offered for money.  
In the rest of the Mundari coun-  
try, called Bhuinharipati, a practice  
has gradually evolved of adopting  
people of reputed members of the  
Kolarian race into a particular s>pt.  
This is oalled Ailire ader or jatire  
ader, When, v. g., Asurs or Mahali  
Mundas ask to be adopted into a  
particular sept, the heads (raje and’  
dewan:) of that sept together with  
those of the other septs established  
in the neighbourhood, hold a pan-  
ebayt and decide whether the re-  
quest is to be granted or rejected  
and fix the sum, if any, to be paid  
for that favour, After this sum  
has been paid and the panches have  
partaken of the banquet provided  
by the petitioners, these are consi-  
dered, atleast by the septs there  
represented, as belonging to the  
Muuda tribe and to the sept of their  
adoption, eo that Mundas of another  
sept, at Jeast of those other septs  
which were represented by their  
panches at the panchayt  
banquet, may henceforth intermarry  
with them without ineurring any  
penalties at the hands of the pan-  
chayt. But many Mundas, even  
of those whose sept was represented,  
continue to consider such people  
stillas doglas and will not readily  
intermarry with them or eat food  
couked or touched by them. In the  
course of time the adoption is likely  
to lapse into oblivion and then the  
difficulties will disappear by them-*  
selves,  
In 1922 the Asur hamlet of Noa-  
tola, near Murumkel, comprising  
some eight or ten families, has been  
thus adopted into the Barla sept.  
But it was in vain that these Asura  
spentsome Rs. 300 over the adop-  
tion. The enlinary members of the  
neighbouring septs, in spite of their  
own panches, consider their adoption  
and -
